Run-and-tumble motion: field theory and entropy production
Rosalba Garcia-Millan, Gunnar Pruessner

Abstract
Run-and-tumble motion is an example of active motility where particles move at constant speed and change direction at random times. In this work we study run-and-tumble motion with diffusion in a harmonic potential in one dimension via a path integral approach. We derive a Doi-Peliti field theory and use it to calculate the entropy production and other observables in closed form. All our results are exact.



1
Introduction

Active matter encompasses reaction-diffusion systems out of equilibrium whose components are
subject to local
non-thermal forces

[
1
]
.
There is a plethora of interesting patterns
and phenomenology in this broad class of systems.
Some fascinating
examples are active nematics
[
2
,
3
,
4
]
,
active emulsions
[
5
]
,
and active motility
[
6
,
7
]
,
amongst many others.
Active matter has become a research focus in statistical mechanics
over recent years,
as it addresses fundamental questions on the physics
of non-equilibrium systems,
but also as a quantitative approach to
biological physics
and the path to designing an autonomous
microbiological engine
[
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
]
.

In this paper we study a model of active motility known as
run and tumble

(RnT) that has been used to describe
bacterial swimming patterns such as that of

Escherichia coli
and
Salmonella

[
12
,
13
]
.
A particle undergoing run-and-tumble motion
moves in a
sequence of
runs
at constant self-propulsion speed
w
interrupted by sudden changes
(
tumbles
) in its orientation
that happen at Poissonian rate
\alpha
,

[
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
,
18
,
19
,
20
,
21
]
.
This motion pattern is ballistic at the microscopic
scale and diffusive at the large scale with effective
diffusion constant
D_{\text{eff}}=w^{2}/\alpha

[
22
,
23
,
24
,
25
,
20
,
12
,
26
,
27
]
.
We study a run-and-tumble particle subject to thermal
noise confined in a harmonic potential
V(x)=kx^{2}/2
,
see figure
1
.

We follow a path integral approach

[
15
]

whereby we derive a perturbative field theory in the Doi-Peliti
framework
[
28
]
and use it to calculate a number of observables in
closed form, with an emphasis on the entropy production

[
29
]
.
Despite our approach being perturbative, all our results are exact.
The presence of the external potential presents technical challenges in the
derivation of the field theory that
resemble those encountered in other contexts
such as the quantum harmonic oscillator
[
30
,
31
,
32
]
.
We use a combination of the Fourier transform and
Hermite polynomials to parametrise the fields as to
diagonalise the action functional.

We regard the Doi-Peliti framework as a method to solve a master equation,
or a Fokker-Planck equation.
It therefore crucially retains
the microscopic dynamics of the system and captures the particle entity.
For this reason, the Doi-Peliti framework provides a solid route to calculate the
entropy production and, most importantly, proves itself to be an effective
tool to study active particle systems.
In this paper, we illustrate this point
by studying RnT motion.

The contents of this paper are organised as follows:
in Sec.
2
, we derive a field theory for an RnT particle in a
harmonic potential;
in Sec.
3
we use this field theory to calculate the entropy
production; and in Sec.
4
we discuss our results.
In the appendix, we have included other relevant observables:
mean square displacement (
C
),
two-point correlation functions (
E
and
F
),
expected velocity (
D
),
and stationary distribution (
G
).

Figure 1
:
Trajectory snapshots
of an RnT particle
in a harmonic potential

V(x)
(solid line). Initially, the
particle at
(x_{0},t_{0})
is right-moving
with positive
velocity
w
(red circles),
until it tumbles at
(x_{1},t_{1})
and
becomes left-moving with
negative velocity
-w
(blue circles).

2
Field theory of run-and-tumble motion with diffusion in a harmonic potential

In one dimension, we can think of run-and-tumble motion as the
interaction between right- and left-moving particles that
transmute into one another at Poissonian rate
\alpha/2
,
see figure
1
.
The Langevin equations of each species
are

\dot{x}_{\phi}=-\partial_{x}V(x)+w+\eta(t)

(1)

for right-moving particles
and

\dot{x}_{\psi}=-\partial_{x}V(x)-w+\eta(t)

(2)

for left-moving particles, where
\eta
is a Gaussian white noise with mean

\left\langle{\eta(t)}\right\rangle=0
and correlation function
\left\langle{\eta(t)\eta(t^{\prime})}\right\rangle=2D\delta(t-t^{\prime})
,
with diffusion constant
D
.
The corresponding Fokker-Plank equations are coupled
due to the transmutation between species through a
gain and loss terms,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}P_{\phi}=-\partial_{x}\left[(-kx+w)P_{\phi}\right]+D\partial_{x}^{2}P_{\phi}+\frac{\alpha}{2}(P_{\psi}-P_{\phi})\,,

(3a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}P_{\psi}=-\partial_{x}\left[(-kx-w)P_{\psi}\right]+D\partial_{x}^{2}P_{\psi}+\frac{\alpha}{2}(P_{\phi}-P_{\psi})\,,

(3b)

where
P_{\phi}(x,t)
and
P_{\psi}(x,t)
are the probability densities of a right- and a left-moving particle
respectively, as a function of position
x
and time
t
. It follows that the action functional
[
39
]
of this process is

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\int\mathchoice{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}\mathchoice{\!\text{d}t\,}{\!\text{d}t\,}{\!\text{d}t\,}{\!\text{d}t\,}\big\{\widetilde{\phi}\left(\partial_{t}\phi+\partial_{x}\left[(-kx+w)\phi\right]-D\partial_{x}^{2}\phi\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\widetilde{\psi}\left(\partial_{t}\psi+\partial_{x}\left[(-kx-w)\psi\right]-D\partial_{x}^{2}\psi\right)+\frac{\alpha}{2}(\widetilde{\phi}-\widetilde{\psi})(\phi-\psi)\big\}\,

(4)

where

\phi
is the annihilation field of right-moving particles;

\widetilde{\phi}
is the Doi-shifted
[
33
]
creation field
\phi^{\dagger}=\widetilde{\phi}+1
of right-moving particles;

\psi
is the annihilation field of left-moving particles;
and

\widetilde{\psi}
is the Doi-shifted creation field
\psi^{\dagger}=\widetilde{\psi}+1
of left-moving particles.
The action functional (
4
) allows the calculation of an observable
\bullet
via the path integral

\left\langle{\bullet}\right\rangle=\int\mathcal{D}[\phi,\widetilde{\phi},\psi,\widetilde{\psi}]\bullet\mathchoice{e^{-\mathcal{A}([\phi,\widetilde{\phi},\psi,\widetilde{\psi}])}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\mathcal{A}([\phi,\widetilde{\phi},\psi,\widetilde{\psi}])\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\mathcal{A}([\phi,\widetilde{\phi},\psi,\widetilde{\psi}])\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\mathcal{A}([\phi,\widetilde{\phi},\psi,\widetilde{\psi}])\right)}\,.

(5)

The action in Equation (
4
) contains only bilinear terms, where the only interaction between
species is due to transmutation. This action does not have any non-linear couplings.
Motivated by the matrix representation of the action
(
4
), we refer to any terms involving

\widetilde{\phi}\phi
or
\widetilde{\psi}\psi
as
diagonal terms, and to the terms
\widetilde{\phi}\psi
and

\widetilde{\psi}\phi
as off-diagonal terms.
The diagonal terms in (
4
) are
semi-local

due to the derivatives in space and time, which need to be made

local
in order to carry out the Gaussian (path) integral.
This is usually achieved by expressing the
fields in Fourier space. In this case, however, Fourier-transforming
(
4
) yields the action local
in the frequency
\omega
but not in reciprocate position
x
.
Due to the diagonal terms
-k\widetilde{\phi}\partial_{x}(x\phi)

-k\widetilde{\psi}\partial_{x}(x\psi)
, the action remains semi-local
after Fourier-transforming.

Instead, we first parametrise the action by the density field

\rho=(\phi+\psi)/\sqrt{2}
and the polarity field

\nu=(\phi-\psi)/\sqrt{2}
, also called
chirality
[
34
]
,
with the analogous transformation for the conjugate fields.
This change of variables is useful in other contexts and is known
under other names, such as the Keldysh rotation
[
35
]
.
In our case, the advantage is that
the action remains invariant under the transformation

\rho\leftrightarrow\nu
,
\widetilde{\rho}\leftrightarrow\widetilde{\nu}
,
except for the mass term
\alpha\widetilde{\nu}\nu
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\int\mathchoice{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}\mathchoice{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\big\{-\mathring{\imath}\omega\widetilde{\rho}\rho-k\widetilde{\rho}\partial_{x}(x\rho)-D\widetilde{\rho}\partial_{x}^{2}\rho

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathring{\imath}\omega\widetilde{\nu}\nu-k\widetilde{\nu}\partial_{x}(x\nu)-D\widetilde{\nu}\partial_{x}^{2}\nu+\alpha\widetilde{\nu}\nu+w\widetilde{\rho}\partial_{x}\nu+w\widetilde{\nu}\partial_{x}\rho\big\}\,.

(6)

We then use the solution to the eigenvalue problem

\mathcal{L}[u(x)]=\lambda u(x)
, with

\mathcal{L}[u(x)]=\partial_{x}^{2}u(x)+\partial_{x}(x\,u(x))/L^{2}\ ,

(7)

L=\sqrt{D/k}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
where the differential operator follows from the diagonal terms
in (
6
).
By using the fields
\rho
and
\nu
,
instead of the original
\phi
and
\psi
, we have the same differential
operator
\mathcal{L}
acting on both

\rho
and
\nu
.

The solution to this eigenvalue problem is
the set of Hermite functions

u_{n}(x)=\mathchoice{e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2L^{2}}}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2L^{2}}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2L^{2}}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2L^{2}}\right)}\,H_{n}\left(\frac{x}{L}\right)\,,

(8)

with eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}=-{n}/{L^{2}}
,
where
H_{n}\left(x\right)
is the
n
-th Hermite polynomial,
see

A

[
36
,
37
,
30
]
.
Defining the set of functions

\widetilde{u}_{n}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{{2\pi}}\,n!}{H_{n}\left(\frac{x}{L}\right)}\,,

(9)

the orthogonality relation between

u_{n}(x)
and
\widetilde{u}_{m}(x)
follows from
the orthogonality
of Hermite polynomials in Equation (
22an
),

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}u_{n}(x)\widetilde{u}_{m}(x)=L\,\delta_{n,m}\,,

(10)

where
\delta_{n,m}
is the Kronecker
\delta
.
We can now use
u_{n}
and
\widetilde{u}_{m}
as basis for the fields

\rho
,
\widetilde{\rho}
,
\nu
and
\widetilde{\nu}
,

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\rho}\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle(\sim)}}(x,t)=\int\mathchoice{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\mathchoice{e^{-\mathring{\imath}\omega t}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}\frac{1}{L}\sum_{n}\mathord{\mathop{\rho}\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle(\sim)}}_{n}(\omega)\mathord{\mathop{u}\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle(\sim)}}_{n}(x)\,,

(11a)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathop{\nu}\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle(\sim)}}(x,t)=\int\mathchoice{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\mathchoice{e^{-\mathring{\imath}\omega t}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}\frac{1}{L}\sum_{n}\mathord{\mathop{\nu}\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle(\sim)}}_{n}(\omega)\mathord{\mathop{u}\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle(\sim)}}_{n}(x)\,.

(11b)

Using the representation (
11
) and the
orthogonality relation (
10
) in the action
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{0}+\mathcal{A}_{1}
in
(
6
),
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{0}=\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{n,m}L\delta_{n,m}\int\mathchoice{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\mathchoice{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}\delta\mkern-8.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega^{\prime})

\displaystyle\qquad\times\big[\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+kn\right)\tilde{\rho}_{n}(\omega^{\prime}){\rho}_{m}(\omega)+\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+kn+\alpha\right)\tilde{\nu}_{n}(\omega^{\prime}){\nu}_{m}(\omega)\big]\,,

(12a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}=-\frac{w}{L}\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{n,m}L\delta_{n-1,m}\int\mathchoice{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\mathchoice{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\text{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}\delta\mkern-8.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega^{\prime})\big[\tilde{\rho}_{n}(\omega^{\prime}){\nu}_{m}(\omega)+\tilde{\nu}_{n}(\omega^{\prime}){\rho}_{m}(\omega)\big]\,,

(12b)

where
\mathcal{A}_{0}
contains the local, diagonal terms
and
\mathcal{A}_{1}
contains the off-diagonal terms, which
are non-local due to
\delta_{n-1,m}
.
We can then regard
\mathcal{A}_{0}
as the Gaussian model and
\mathcal{A}_{1}

as a perturbation about it.

The Gaussian model corresponds to
Ornstein-Uhlenbeck particles where one species has
decay rate
\alpha
.
Defining an observable
\bullet
in
the Gaussian model as

\left\langle{\bullet}\right\rangle_{0}=\int\mathcal{D}[\rho,\widetilde{\rho},\nu,\widetilde{\nu}]\bullet\mathchoice{e^{-\mathcal{A}_{0}([\rho,\widetilde{\rho},\nu,\widetilde{\nu}])}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\mathcal{A}_{0}([\rho,\widetilde{\rho},\nu,\widetilde{\nu}])\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\mathcal{A}_{0}([\rho,\widetilde{\rho},\nu,\widetilde{\nu}])\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\mathcal{A}_{0}([\rho,\widetilde{\rho},\nu,\widetilde{\nu}])\right)}\,,

(13)

from (
5
) it follows that
the perturbation expansion of the
observable in the full model, the RnT particle,
is

\left\langle{\bullet}\right\rangle=\left\langle{\bullet\mathchoice{e^{-\mathcal{A}_{1}}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\mathcal{A}_{1}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\mathcal{A}_{1}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\mathcal{A}_{1}\right)}}\right\rangle_{0}=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{N!}\left\langle{\bullet(-\mathcal{A}_{1})^{N}}\right\rangle_{0}\,.

(14)

By performing the Gaussian path integral
[
38
]
in (
13
),
the bare propagators read

\displaystyle\hbox to76.63pt{\vbox to17.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 36.98048pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

(15a)

\displaystyle\hbox to76.52pt{\vbox to18.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 36.86531pt\lower-1.92264pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

(15b)

where

when calculating any observable.
We use Feynman diagrams to represent propagators
[

,

]

where time (causality) is read from right to left.
On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\langle{\rho_{n}(\omega)\tilde{\nu}_{m}(\omega^{\prime})}\rangle_{0}=\,0\,,

(16a)

\displaystyle\langle{\nu_{n}(\omega)\tilde{\rho}_{m}(\omega^{\prime})}\rangle_{0}=\,0\,,

2.1
Full propagator in reciprocate space

To calculate certain observables such as the time-dependent
probability distribution of the RnT particle,
we need the full propagators.
To derive the full propagators we use
the action in (
12
), the bare propagators in
(
15
), (
16
), the perturbative vertex (
17
), as well as Wick’s Theorem
[
38
]
.
Consider, for instance, the propagator of the density field

\left\langle{\rho\widetilde{\rho}}\right\rangle
. From (
14
) we have

\hbox to67.94pt{\vbox to19.73pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 32.72871pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

\displaystyle=\frac{w^{2}}{L}\frac{\delta_{n,m+2}\delta\mkern-8.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega^{\prime})}{\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+k(m+2)+r\right)\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+k(m+1)+\alpha\right)\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+km+r\right)}\,,

using two of the index-shifting vertices (

).
From (
16a
) it follows
that any term in (
18
) of odd order
N
vanishes because the fields
\rho
,
\tilde{\rho}
,

\nu
and
\tilde{\nu}
cannot be paired according to
(

). Then, the contributions to the full propagator

\left\langle{\rho\widetilde{\rho}}\right\rangle
are

\frac{1}{N!}\left\langle{\rho_{n}(\omega)\widetilde{\rho}_{m}(\omega^{\prime})(-\mathcal{A}_{1})^{N}}\right\rangle_{0}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}L\delta_{n,m+N}\delta\mkern-8.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega^{\prime})\left(\frac{w}{L}\right)^{N}\prod_{j=0}^{N}\frac{1}{-\mathring{\imath}\omega+k(m+j)+p_{j}}&\text{if }N\text{ even}\,,\\
0&\text{if }N\text{ odd}\,,\end{array}\right.

(21)

where
p_{j}=r
if
j
is even and
p_{j}=\alpha
if
j
is odd.
Similarly, the contributions to the full propagators

\left\langle{\nu\widetilde{\nu}}\right\rangle
,
\left\langle{\rho\widetilde{\nu}}\right\rangle
and
\left\langle{\nu\widetilde{\rho}}\right\rangle

are, respectively,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N!}\left\langle{\nu_{n}(\omega)\widetilde{\nu}_{m}(\omega^{\prime})(-\mathcal{A}_{1})^{N}}\right\rangle_{0}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}L\delta_{n,m+N}\delta\mkern-8.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega^{\prime})\left(\frac{w}{L}\right)^{N}\prod_{j=0}^{N}\frac{1}{-\mathring{\imath}\omega+k(m+j)+q_{j}}&\text{if }N\text{ even}\,,\\
0&\text{if }N\text{ odd}\,,\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N!}\left\langle{\rho_{n}(\omega)\widetilde{\nu}_{m}(\omega^{\prime})(-\mathcal{A}_{1})^{N}}\right\rangle_{0}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}0&\text{if }N\text{ even}\,,\\
L\delta_{n,m+N}\delta\mkern-8.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega^{\prime})\left(\frac{w}{L}\right)^{N}\prod_{j=0}^{N}\frac{1}{-\mathring{\imath}\omega+k(m+j)+q_{j}}&\text{if }N\text{ odd}\,,\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N!}\left\langle{\nu_{n}(\omega)\widetilde{\rho}_{m}(\omega^{\prime})(-\mathcal{A}_{1})^{N}}\right\rangle_{0}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}0&\text{if }N\text{ even}\,,\\
L\delta_{n,m+N}\delta\mkern-8.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega^{\prime})\left(\frac{w}{L}\right)^{N}\prod_{j=0}^{N}\frac{1}{-\mathring{\imath}\omega+k(m+j)+p_{j}}&\text{if }N\text{ odd}\,,\end{array}\right.

where
q_{j}=\alpha
if
j
is even and
q_{j}=r
if
j
is odd.

(22wd)

where the black circle

represents the sum over all possible diagrams that have the
same incoming and outgoing legs.
Some of these propagators are calculated in closed form in
B
.

2.2
Short-time propagator in real space

In this section we calculate the
short-time propagator
\langle{\phi(y,\tau)\widetilde{\phi}(x,0)}\rangle_{1}

of a
right-moving particle that moves from
position
x
to
y
in an interval of
time
\tau
,
and the
short-time propagator
\langle{\psi(y,\tau)\widetilde{\phi}(x,0)}\rangle_{1}

of a
right-moving particle
x
that transmutates
into a left-moving particle at
y

in an interval of
time
\tau
.
The subindex indicates that the propagator is
expanded to first order about the Gaussian model,

\langle{\bullet}\rangle_{1}=\langle{\bullet}\rangle_{0}-\langle{\bullet\mathcal{A}_{1}}\rangle_{0}
. Expanding to
N
th order in the perturbative part of the action generally provides the
N
th order in
\tau

[
39
]
.
Using the recurrence relation (
22ap
),
Mehler’s formula (
22aq
) and
the propagators in Eqs. (
22ar
)–(
22aw
),
these two propagators are,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\hbox to33.01pt{\vbox to1pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 16.50467pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

\lxSVG@closescope }

(22xa)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{k}{2\pi D\left(1-\mathchoice{e^{-2k\tau}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2k\tau\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2k\tau\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2k\tau\right)}\right)}}\mathchoice{e^{-\frac{k(y-x\mathchoice{e^{-k\tau}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-k\tau\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-k\tau\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-k\tau\right)})^{2}}{2D\left(1-\mathchoice{e^{-2k\tau}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2k\tau\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2k\tau\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2k\tau\right)}\right)}}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{k(y-x\mathchoice{e^{-k\tau}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-k\tau\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-k\tau\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-k\tau\right)})^{2}}{2D\left(1-\mathchoice{e^{-2k\tau}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2k\tau\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2k\tau\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2k\tau\right)}\right)}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{k(y-x\mathchoice{e^{-k\tau}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-k\tau\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-k\tau\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-k\tau\right)})^{2}}{2D\left(1-\mathchoice{e^{-2k\tau}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2k\tau\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2k\tau\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2k\tau\right)}\right)}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{k(y-x\mathchoice{e^{-k\tau}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-k\tau\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-k\tau\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-k\tau\right)})^{2}}{2D\left(1-\mathchoice{e^{-2k\tau}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2k\tau\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2k\tau\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2k\tau\right)}\right)}\right)}

(22xf)

) with (
22xf
)
we see that the transition probability that involves exactly one
transmutation event, independently of displacement, is
of higher order in
\tau
than the transition probability of
just a displacement.

2.3
Zeroth, first and second moments of the
position of a right-moving particle

Figure 2
:

Conditional expected position
\left\langle{x_{\phi}(t)}\right\rangle/\left\langle{x_{\phi}^{0}(t)}\right\rangle
of a right-moving RnT particle in a harmonic potential
, Eqs. (
22za
) and (
22zb
), for a range of

w
and
x_{0}
, with
\alpha=1
and
k=w/\xi
with
\xi=1
.

As it will become clear in Sec.
3
,
we need the zeroth, first and second moments of the position
of a right-moving particle to calculate the entropy production
of an RnT particle in a harmonic potential.
Assuming that the system is initialised
with a right-moving particle placed at
x_{0}
at time
t_{0}=0
,
the
n
th moment of its position is

\left\langle{x^{n}_{\phi}(t)}\right\rangle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}x^{n}\langle{\phi(x,t)\widetilde{\phi}(x_{0},0)}\rangle\,,

(22y)

where the propagator
\langle{\phi(x,t)\widetilde{\phi}(x_{0},0)}\rangle

expressed in terms of the
\mathord{\mathop{\rho}\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle(\sim)}}
and
\mathord{\mathop{\nu}\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle(\sim)}}
fields
contains the four full propagators,

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.97 0 C 1.97 1.09 1.09 1.97 0 1.97 C -1.09 1.97 -1.97 1.09 -1.97 0 C -1.97 -1.09 -1.09 -1.97 0 -1.97 C 1.09 -1.97 1.97 -1.09 1.97 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

, Equation (
22w
).
Definition (
22y
) contains a mild abuse of notation that we will keep committing throughout, as the angular brackets were introduced in (
5
) as a path integral but are expectation over a density in (
22y
).
Since there is an integral over space and
x^{n}

is a polynomial in
x
, the observable can be written
as linear combinations of Hermite polynomials, which simplifies
the calculations by virtue of the orthogonality relation in
(
22an
). In particular, for the first three moments,

1=H_{0}\left(x/L\right)
,

x/L=H_{1}\left(x/L\right)
and

(x/L)^{2}=H_{0}\left(x/L\right)+H_{2}\left(x/L\right)
.
Using the representation in (
11
) and the
propagators derived in
B
, the
zeroth, first (see figure
2
) and second moments are

(22zc)

From the propagators in Eqs. (
22ar
)–(
22aw
),
we see that at stationarity, only those terms remain where
the Doi-shifted creation field is

\widetilde{\rho}_{0}
. Then, Eqs. (
22z
) simplify to

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\left\langle{x_{\phi}^{0}(t)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}

(22aaa)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\left\langle{x_{\phi}^{1}(t)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{w}{k+\alpha}

(22aab)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\left\langle{x_{\phi}^{2}(t)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{D}{2k}+\frac{w^{2}}{2k(k+\alpha)}\,

(22aac)

at stationarity.

3
Entropy production

In this section we derive the internal entropy production

\dot{S}_{i}
at stationarity
[
40
,
41
,
42
,
43
]
.
Other observables we calculate
are in the appendix:
mean square displacement (
C
),
two-point correlation function (
E
),
two-time correlation function (
F
),
expected velocity (
D
) and
stationary distribution (
G
).

The internal entropy production
is defined as
[
44
,
40
,
39
]

\dot{S}_{i}(t)=\lim_{\tau\to 0}\frac{1}{\tau}\int\mathchoice{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}\mathchoice{\!\text{d}y\,}{\!\text{d}y\,}{\!\text{d}y\,}{\!\text{d}y\,}P(x,t)W(x\to y;\tau)\log\left(\frac{P(x,t)W(x\to y;\tau)}{P(y,t)W(y\to x;\tau)}\right)\,,

(22ab)

where
P(x,t)
is the probability that the system is
in state
x
at time
t
and

W(x\to y;\tau)
is the transition probability of the system to change from state

x
to
y
in an interval of time

\tau
.
The internal entropy production rate
\dot{S}_{i}

is non-negative, and it is zero if and only if the
detailed balance condition

P(x)W(x\to y)=P(y)W(y\to x)
is satisfied for any
two pairs of states
x
and
y
.
A positive entropy production rate is thus the signature
of non-equilibrium and it indicates the breakdown of
time-reversal symmetry.
The entropy production
\dot{S}_{i}(t)

of a drift-diffusive particle in free space with
velocity
w
and diffusion constant
D
is known
to be
\dot{S}_{i}(t)=1/(2t)+w^{2}/D

[
40
]
, where the first contribution
is due to the relaxation to steady state and is
independent of the system parameters, and the second
contribution is due to the steady-state probability
current.

We can anticipate that the stationary entropy production
rate
\dot{S}_{i}
of an RnT particle in a
harmonic potential is positive given that, between
tumbles, the particle is drift-diffusive and, therefore,
there is locally a perpetual current. Moreover, a
particle’s forward trajectory, such as in figure
1
,
is distinct from its backwards trajectory, which
indicates the breakdown of time-reversal symmetry.

Given the RnT particle is confined in a potential, its probability distribution develops into a stationary state,
see
G
. We denote the stationary distribution by

P(x)=\lim_{t\to\infty}P(x,t)
. In the limit

t\to\infty
, the following identity holds for a Markov
process

\int\mathchoice{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}W(x\to y;\tau)P(x)=P(y)\,.

(22ac)

Using (
22ac
), the Markovian property

\int\mathchoice{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}W(y\to x;\tau)=1\,,

(22ad)

and the convention

{W}(y\to x;0)=\delta(x-y)
,
Equation (
22ab
) at stationarity simplifies to

\lim_{t\to\infty}\dot{S}_{i}(t)=\lim_{\tau\to 0}\int\mathchoice{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}\mathchoice{\!\text{d}y\,}{\!\text{d}y\,}{\!\text{d}y\,}{\!\text{d}y\,}P(x)\dot{W}(x\to y;\tau)\log\left(\frac{W(x\to y;\tau)}{W(y\to x;\tau)}\right)\,.

(22ae)

Since the entropy production involves the limit

\tau\to 0
of the transition rate
\dot{W}=\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}\tau}W
,
the entropy production crucially draws on the
microscopic dynamics of the process. We can therefore
focus on lower order contributions to
\dot{W}
in

\tau
and neglect higher order contributions.

For an RnT particle, all possible transitions
between states involve a displacement (run) and/or
a change in the direction of the drift (tumble).
Given that, at stationarity, there is a symmetry
between right- and left-moving particles, we can
summarise the contributions to the entropy production
(
22ae
) as

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\dot{S}_{i}(t)=\lim_{\tau\to 0}2\int\mathchoice{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}\mathchoice{\!\text{d}y\,}{\!\text{d}y\,}{\!\text{d}y\,}{\!\text{d}y\,}P_{\phi}(x)\left[\dot{W}(x\to y,\phi;\tau)\log\left(\frac{W(x\to y,\phi;\tau)}{W(y\to x,\phi;\tau)}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\dot{W}(x\to y,\phi\to\psi;\tau)\log\left(\frac{W(x\to y,\phi\to\psi;\tau)}{W(y\to x,\psi\to\phi;\tau)}\right)\right]\,,

(22af)

where
P_{\phi}(x)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\langle{\phi(x,t)\widetilde{\phi}(x_{0},0)}\rangle
.
The first term in the square bracket in (
22af
) corresponds to the displacement of a right-moving particle from
x
to
y

with transition probability

W(x\to y,\phi;\tau)=\langle{\phi(y,\tau)\widetilde{\phi}(x,0)}\rangle
.
The second term in the square bracket of (
22af
)
corresponds to the displacement of a particle
from
x
to
y
that starts as right-moving and
ends as left-moving, with transition probability

W(x\to y,\phi\to\psi;\tau)=\langle{\psi(y,\tau)\widetilde{\phi}(x,0)}\rangle
.
These two transitions include any intermediate state where there may
be displacement and transmutation,
although their transition probabilities are of
higher order in
\tau
and therefore can be
neglected. We therefore use the short-time propagators in (
22x
)
[
40
,
39
]
.

In the following, we analyse which terms in (
22af
) contribute
to the entropy production rate
\dot{S}_{i}
. We first consider the
transition due to transmutation and displacement, whose short-time propagator is
(
22xf
). By symmetry, we see that
the transition
probability corresponding to displacement
and transmutation in the short-time limit is

W(x\to y,\phi\to\psi;\tau)=W(y\to x,\psi\to\phi;\tau)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi D\tau}}\mathchoice{e^{-\frac{(y-x)^{2}}{4D\tau}}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{(y-x)^{2}}{4D\tau}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{(y-x)^{2}}{4D\tau}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{(y-x)^{2}}{4D\tau}\right)}\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}\tau+\mathcal{O}\left(\tau^{2}\right)\right)\,,

(22ag)

which is equal for the forward and backward trajectories. As the
logarithm vanishes, the second
term in (
22af
) does not contribute.

The transition associated to
the displacement of a right-moving particle from
x
to
y

is similar to that of a drift-diffusive
particle in a harmonic potential, so we expect the first term in
(
22af
)
to contribute to the entropy production.
Using the short-time propagator of a right-moving
particle

W(x\to y,\phi;\tau)\simeq\langle{\phi(y,\tau)\widetilde{\phi}(x,0)}\rangle_{1}

in (
22xb
),
the first logarithm in Equation (
22af
) is,

\lim_{\tau\to 0}\log\left(\frac{\langle{\phi(y,\tau)\widetilde{\phi}(x,0)}\rangle_{1}}{\langle{\phi(x,\tau)\widetilde{\phi}(y,0)}\rangle_{1}}\right)=\frac{y-x}{D}\left(w-k\frac{x+y}{2}\right)\,.

(22ah)

From the short-time propagator in (
22xc
) and,
equivalently, from the Fokker-Planck equation (
3a
), the kernel

\dot{W}

that we need in (
22af
) is

\lim_{\tau\to 0}\dot{W}(x\to y,\phi;\tau)=D\delta^{\prime\prime}(x-y)-(w-ky)\delta^{\prime}(x-y)-\frac{\alpha}{2}\delta(x-y)\,.

(22ai)

Figure 3
:

Stationary probability density and local entropy production rate
\sigma(x)
of a right-moving (R) and left-moving (L) particle
,
Eqs. (
22ak
), (
22bm
) and (
22bq
),
with
D=0.01
,
\alpha=2
.
In (a) and (d)
w=2
,
k=2
;
in (b) and (e)
w=1
,
k=1
;
and in (c) and (f)
w=0.5
,
k=0.5
.
We used multiple-precision floating-point arithmetic

[
45
]
to implement Hermite polynomials
up to
H_{10^{4}}\left(x\right)

based on the GNU Scientific Library implementation

[
46
]
, see figure
6
.

Using (
22ah
) and (
22ai
), Equation (
22af
) simplifies to

\dot{S}_{i}=2\int\mathchoice{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}P_{\phi}(x)\left(\frac{1}{D}(w-kx)^{2}-k\right)\,,

(22aj)

which shows that the local entropy production rate
[
29
,
47
]

for right-moving particles is

\sigma_{\phi}(x)=\frac{1}{D}(w-kx)^{2}-k\,,

(22ak)

and
\sigma_{\psi}(x)=\sigma_{\phi}(-x)

for left-moving particles, see figure
3
.
The local entropy production rate
\sigma_{\phi}
is minimal
at the characteristic point
\xi=w/k
, where a right-moving particle has a zero expected
velocity
because the self-propulsion equals the force exerted
by the potential,
and its motion is entirely due to the thermal
noise.
We can calculate (
22aj
)
using the moments derived in
Sec.
2.3
.
On the basis of Eqs. (
22aa
), the total stationary internal
entropy production
is

\dot{S}_{i}=\frac{\alpha w^{2}}{D(k+\alpha)}\,,

(22al)

see figure
4
.

Figure 4
:

Stationary internal
entropy production rate
of an RnT particle in a harmonic potential
as a function of
w
, Equation (
22al
),
with
\alpha=2
,
D=0.01
,
\xi=1
and varying
k
.
The points where the grey line crosses the
lines with
k=0.5
,
k=1
and
k=2
give

\dot{S}_{i}

in the examples shown in figure
3
.

4
Discussion and conclusions

In this paper we have used the Doi-Peliti framework to describe an RnT particle in a harmonic potential and
calculate its entropy production (
22al
)
and other observables (such as (
22x
), (
22aa
),
(
22bb
),
(
22be
),
(
22bg
),
(
22bj
), (
22bm
)
and
(
22bq
))
in closed form.
The key result in Equation (
22al
) shows that the stationary internal
entropy production of an RnT particle is proportional
to that of a drift-diffusive particle in free space, where

\dot{S}_{i}=w^{2}/D

[
40
]
.
In the presence of an external harmonic potential (
k>0
),
the entropy production is always smaller than that of a free particle.
If there is no tumbling (
\alpha=0
), then the system is an
Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process, which is at equilibrium
and therefore produces no entropy.

The positive entropy production rate implies the breaking
of time symmetry whereby forward and backward trajectories
are distinguishable
[
40
]
. This is visible in the
trajectory of an RnT particle, such as in figure
1
,
where we can see that the particle runs fast
when "going down" the potential and it slows down as it
moves up the steep slope of the potential.

The present example of an RnT particle illustrates the power of field theories that
capture the microscopic dynamics to deal with active systems.
Since the large scale behaviour of an RnT particle
is that of a diffusive particle,
by studying an effective theory that captures the large scale
only, we would obtain that the entropy production is zero,
in contradiction with our result (
22al
).

Finally, deriving the Doi-Peliti field theory
presents an important technical challenge. Due to the external
harmonic potential, the action functional is semi-local
both in real space and in Fourier space. Instead, to diagonalise the action we decomposed
the fields in a basis of Hermite polynomials following the spirit of
the harmonic oscillator
[
37
]
.

We would like to thank Umut Özer for his contribution to an earlier exploration on the field theory of an RnT particle in a harmonic potential. We are grateful to Ziluo Zhang for many enlightening discussions about the field theory of an RnT particle in two dimensions. We would also like to thank Marius Bothe, Benjamin Walter, Zigan Zhen, Luca Cocconi, Frédéric van Wijland, Yuting Irene Li, Patrick Pietzonka and Davide Venturelli for fruitful discussions.
This work was funded in part by the European Research Council under the EU’s Horizon 2020 Programme, grant number 740269.

Appendix A
Hermite polynomials

The definition of Hermite polynomials
[
48
]

we use in this paper is

H_{n}\left(x\right)=(-1)^{n}\,\mathchoice{e^{\frac{x^{2}}{2}}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)}\,\frac{\text{d}^{n}}{\text{d}x^{n}}\left(\mathchoice{e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)}\right)\,,

(22am)

where
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.
Some of the properties
of Hermite polynomials that we use
are listed in the following

[
48
]
.

Orthogonality:

Hermite polynomials are orthogonal with respect to the weight function
f(x)=\mathchoice{e^{-{x^{2}}/{2}}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-{x^{2}}/{2}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-{x^{2}}/{2}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-{x^{2}}/{2}\right)}
,

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}\mathchoice{e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)}H_{n}\left(x\right)H_{m}\left(x\right)=\sqrt{2\pi}\,n!\,\delta_{n,m}\,,

(22an)

where
\delta_{n,m}
is the Kronecker delta.

Hermite’s differential equation:

Hermite polynomials
H_{n}\left(x\right)
are eigenfunctions of the differential operator

H^{\prime\prime}-xH^{\prime}=-\mu H\,,

(22ao)

for non-negative integer eigenvalues
\mu_{n}=n
.
Using this equation, we can show that the
Hermite functions
u_{n}(x)=f(x/L)H_{n}\left(x/L\right)
are solution
to the eigenvalue problem

\mathcal{L}[u]=u^{\prime\prime}+(x\,u)^{\prime}/L^{2}=\lambda u
in Sec.
2
, Equation (
7
), with eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}=-n/L^{2}

[
49
]
.

Recurrence relation:

Hermite polynomials satisfy the recurrence relation

H_{n+1}\left(x\right)=xH_{n}\left(x\right)-H_{n}^{\prime}(x)\,.

(22ap)

Mehler’s formula:

Hermite polynomials satisfy the following identity

[
48
,
37
]
,

\mathchoice{e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{s^{n}}{n!}H_{n}\left(x\right)H_{n}\left(y\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-s^{2}}}\mathchoice{e^{-\frac{(x-ys)^{2}}{2\left(1-s^{2}\right)}}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{(x-ys)^{2}}{2\left(1-s^{2}\right)}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{(x-ys)^{2}}{2\left(1-s^{2}\right)}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\frac{(x-ys)^{2}}{2\left(1-s^{2}\right)}\right)}\,.

(22aq)

(22aw)

after letting
r\to 0
. These propagators are then used
to calculate the full propagator in
real space via

\langle{\rho(x,t)\widetilde{\rho}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})}\rangle=\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{n,m,N}u_{n}(x)\widetilde{u}_{m}(x^{\prime})\frac{1}{N!}\left\langle{\rho_{n}(t)\widetilde{\rho}_{m}(t^{\prime})(-\mathcal{A}_{1})^{N}}\right\rangle_{0}\,,

(22ax)

where
\mathord{\mathop{\rho}\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle(\sim)}}
may be replaced by
\mathord{\mathop{\nu}\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle(\sim)}}
.
The stationary distribution is derived from this expression in
G
.

Appendix C
Mean square displacement

The mean square displacement is defined
as

R^{2}(t)=\left\langle{\left(x(t)-x_{0}\right)^{2}}\right\rangle\,.

(22ay)

Assuming that the system is initialised
with a right-moving particle, the propagator
that probes for
any
particle at a later
time is

\big\langle{\big(\phi(x,t)+\psi(x,t)\big)\widetilde{\phi}(x_{0},0)}\big\rangle
.
Following the same scheme as in
Sec.
2.3
, the first and second
moments of the position of an RnT particle are

\displaystyle\left\langle{x(t)}\right\rangle=x_{0}\mathchoice{e^{-kt}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-kt\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-kt\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-kt\right)}+\frac{w}{k-\alpha}\left(\mathchoice{e^{-\alpha t}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\alpha t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\alpha t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-\alpha t\right)}-\mathchoice{e^{-kt}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-kt\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-kt\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-kt\right)}\right)\,,

(22az)

\displaystyle\left\langle{x^{2}(t)}\right\rangle=x_{0}^{2}\mathchoice{e^{-2kt}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt\right)}+\frac{D}{k}\left(1-\mathchoice{e^{-2kt}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt\right)}\right)+2\frac{x_{0}w}{k-\alpha}\left(\mathchoice{e^{-(k+\alpha)t}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)t\right)}-\mathchoice{e^{-2kt}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt\right)}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+2w^{2}\left(\frac{\mathchoice{e^{-2kt}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt\right)}}{2k(k-\alpha)}+\frac{\mathchoice{e^{-(k+\alpha)t}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)t\right)}}{(k+\alpha)(\alpha-k)}+\frac{1}{2k(k+\alpha)}\right)\,.

(22ba)

Then, the mean square displacement at stationarity
is

\lim_{t\to\infty}R^{2}(t)=x_{0}^{2}+\frac{D}{k}+\frac{w^{2}}{k(k+\alpha)}\,,

(22bb)

see figure
5(a)
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 5
:
(
5(a)
)
Mean square displacement
R^{2}(t)

and
(
5(b)
) expected velocity

\left\langle{\mathcal{W}_{\phi}(t)}\right\rangle

of an RnT particle in a harmonic potential.
In
(
5(a)
)
,

x_{0}=0.5
,
\alpha=1
,
k=w/\xi

with
\xi=1
, and a range of
D
,
using
Eqs. (
22ay
), (
22az
) and (
22ba
).
In
(
5(b)
)
,

k=0.5
,
\alpha=1
and a range of
x_{0}
and
w
,
using
Eqs. (
22bd
), (
22za
) and (
22zb
). The dotted lines show the particle’s self-propulsion speeds
w
, which are the instantaneous velocities at the origin.

Appendix D
Expected velocity

To calculate the expected velocity of a right-moving
particle, one could naïvely differentiate the expected position
\left\langle{x_{\phi}(t)}\right\rangle

in Equation (
22zb
) with respect to time,

\partial_{t}\left\langle{x_{\phi}(t)}\right\rangle=\int\mathchoice{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}x\,\partial_{t}P_{\phi}(x,t)\,.

(22bc)

However, this expression fails to capture the
expected velocity because in the limit

t\to\infty
, the stationary distribution
satisfies
\partial_{t}P_{\phi}(x,t)=0
, implying that the result
in (
22bc
) is zero. This is in
contradiction with the nature of an RnT particle,
which has a perpetual non-zero drift.
Ultimately, the ambiguity in the definition of the velocity is a matter of Ito versus Stratonovich, namely to consider a particle’s displacement conditional to its point of departure (Ito), its point of arrival or the average of the two (Stratonovich).

Instead, to calculate the expected velocity of a right-moving particle
\left\langle{\mathcal{W}_{\phi}(t)}\right\rangle
we
draw on its local velocity, which is
w-kx
given its position
x
prior to its instantaneous departure, as captured by the Fokker-Planck Equation (
3a
).
The expected velocity
is then the conditional expectation

\left\langle{\mathcal{W}_{\phi}(t)}\right\rangle=\frac{\int\mathchoice{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}(w-kx)\langle{\phi(x,t)\widetilde{\phi}(x_{0},t_{0})}\rangle}{\int\mathchoice{\!\text{d}x^{\prime}\,}{\!\text{d}x^{\prime}\,}{\!\text{d}x^{\prime}\,}{\!\text{d}x^{\prime}\,}\langle{\phi(x^{\prime},t)\widetilde{\phi}(x_{0},t_{0})}\rangle}\,,

(22bd)

which, using Eqs. (
22za
) and (
22zb
), is, at stationarity

\lim_{t\to\infty}\left\langle{\mathcal{W}_{\phi}(t)}\right\rangle=\frac{\alpha w}{k+\alpha}\,,

(22be)

see figure
5(b)
.

Appendix E
Two-point correlation function

The two-point correlation function
\mathcal{F}(x,y;t)

is the observable

\mathcal{F}(x,y;t)=\big\langle{\big(\phi(x,t)+\psi(x,t)\big)\big(\phi(y,t)+\psi(y,t)\big)\widetilde{\phi}(x_{0},0)}\big\rangle+\big\langle{\big(\phi(x,t)+\psi(x,t)\big)\widetilde{\phi}(x_{0},0)}\big\rangle\delta(x-y)\,,

(22bf)

where, after placing a right-moving
particle at
x_{0}
at time
t_{0}
, the system is probed for
any

particle at positions
x
and
y
simultaneously.
The second term contributing only when
x=y
has its origin in the commutation relation of the creation and annihilation operators
[
33
]
.
Since there is exactly
one RnT particle in the system, it cannot be in two
different positions at the same time and therefore
\mathcal{F}(x,y;t)=0

for all
t
when
x\neq y
.
Diagrammatically, (
22bf
) may be written as

\mathcal{F}(x,y;t)\hat{=}\sqrt{2}\left(\hbox to43.68pt{\vbox to14.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 21.83957pt\lower-7.31319pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

(22bg)

which is zero at
x\neq y
due to a lack of a vertex

,
i.e.
due to
the impossibility
of joining a single incoming leg with two out-going legs
because there is no suitable vertex available.
This is an example of how a
Doi-Peliti field theory retains the particle
entity. At
x=y
the two-point correlation function reduces to the propagators Eqs. (
22wa
) and (
22wc
).

Appendix F
Two-time correlation function

The correlation function
\left\langle{x(t)x(t^{\prime})}\right\rangle
, with
t_{0}<t^{\prime}<t
, is given by the observable

\left\langle{x(t)x(t^{\prime})}\right\rangle=\int\mathchoice{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}{\!\text{d}x\,}\mathchoice{\!\text{d}x^{\prime}\,}{\!\text{d}x^{\prime}\,}{\!\text{d}x^{\prime}\,}{\!\text{d}x^{\prime}\,}x\,x^{\prime}\mathcal{G}(x,x^{\prime},x_{0};t,t^{\prime},t_{0})\,,

(22bh)

where the "propagator" is now

\mathcal{G}(x,x^{\prime},x_{0};t,t^{\prime},t_{0})=\big\langle\big[\phi(x,t)+\psi(x,t)\big]\big[\widetilde{\phi}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\phi(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})+\widetilde{\psi}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\psi(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\big]\widetilde{\phi}(x_{0},t_{0})\big\rangle\,.

(22bi)

This propagator indicates that the system is initialised with a right-moving particle at
x_{0}
at time
t_{0}=0
, and it is let to evolve by an interval of time
t^{\prime}-t_{0}
.
At time
t^{\prime}
, the propagator probes for the presence of a particle at

x^{\prime}
, which involves its annihilation and immediate re-creation.
The system is then let to evolve a further interval of time
t-t^{\prime}
, at which point the presence
of either species is measured again at position
x
.
Following the same procedure as in
Sec.
2.3
,
the two-time correlation function reads

\displaystyle\left\langle{x(t)x(t^{\prime})}\right\rangle=\,\mathchoice{e^{-k(t-t^{\prime})}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-k(t-t^{\prime})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-k(t-t^{\prime})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-k(t-t^{\prime})\right)}\left[x_{0}^{2}\mathchoice{e^{-2kt^{\prime}}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt^{\prime}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt^{\prime}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt^{\prime}\right)}+\frac{D}{k}\left(1-\mathchoice{e^{-2kt^{\prime}}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt^{\prime}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt^{\prime}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt^{\prime}\right)}\right)+2\frac{x_{0}w}{k-\alpha}\left(\mathchoice{e^{-(k+\alpha)t^{\prime}}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)t^{\prime}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)t^{\prime}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)t^{\prime}\right)}-\mathchoice{e^{-2kt^{\prime}}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt^{\prime}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt^{\prime}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt^{\prime}\right)}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+2w^{2}\left(\mathchoice{e^{-2kt^{\prime}}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt^{\prime}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt^{\prime}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-2kt^{\prime}\right)}\frac{1}{2k(k-\alpha)}+\mathchoice{e^{-(k+\alpha)t^{\prime}}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)t^{\prime}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)t^{\prime}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)t^{\prime}\right)}\frac{1}{(k+\alpha)(\alpha-k)}+\frac{1}{2k(k+\alpha)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{w}{k+\alpha}\left(1-\mathchoice{e^{-(k+\alpha)(t-t^{\prime})}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)(t-t^{\prime})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)(t-t^{\prime})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)(t-t^{\prime})\right)}\right)\left[\frac{w}{k+\alpha}\left(1-\mathchoice{e^{-(k+\alpha)t^{\prime}}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)t^{\prime}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)t^{\prime}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)t^{\prime}\right)}\right)+x_{0}\mathchoice{e^{-(k+\alpha)t^{\prime}}}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)t^{\prime}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)t^{\prime}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\!\left(-(k+\alpha)t^{\prime}\right)}\right]\,,

(22bj)

see
[
22
]

for details.

Stationary distribution

.

, which are needed when the perturbative prefactor

in Equation (

) is large.
